--- Page 1 ---
<meta>This page concludes a practical recipe for producing white, translucent porcelain—likely a European attempt to mimic "Chinaware"—and transitions into a new title page for a work on Eirenaeus Philalethes by Johann Michael Faust.</meta>

Increase the heat for 24 hours so that the furnace glows white-hot; the longer it stands, the better it becomes. Then, break open the crucible; the substance will be white glass, colored like milk. It should be ground and rubbed on marble with water until it is like milk or lead-white <note>original: "Bleyweiß", a common white pigment in the 18th century</note>. Afterward, one should make small vessels from white earth <note>clay</note> that can withstand fire and becomes entirely white, such as the clay from Tournai <note>original: "Dornickische", referring to the city of Tournai, Belgium, famous for its clay</note> which is reddish-grey but turns completely white when fired. These vessels should be made very thin on the potter’s wheel or bench and shaved; they can also be pressed into molds while they are still soft. Once they have become thin enough, they are thoroughly soaked with the previously described varnish <note>The author uses "Firnuß" (varnish) to mean a liquid glaze</note> and become completely translucent. However, where the vessels are thicker, one must work a sixth part of the glass-varnish or glaze into the clay itself. Likewise, the earthenware vessels must be "cooked" <note>pre-fired or bisque-fired</note> in a low fire before they are glazed, inside a protective casing <note>a sagger, used to protect delicate ceramics in the kiln</note> in which each small vessel stands enclosed on three feet or small points made of the same white earth. This casing must be made of Namur clay <note>clay from the Namur region of modern Belgium, known for heat resistance</note> and have all its joints well sealed. Once it has been cooked for 24 hours with a "dark glow" <note>a low-heat firing</note>, one should take the pots or vessels out, shave them as needed, and glaze them with the previously mentioned milk-powder mixed with water. This is done so that the water runs off, and the glaze should be as thick as half a *Reichs-orth* <term>a quarter-thaler coin, approximately 1 millimeter thick</term> to penetrate the vessels. When they are dry, one should paint them with enamel or blue pigment <note>original: "Blau Ratzer"</note> on the wheel or bench, in the manner of Chinese figures and vessels. As soon as they have dried again, they should be packed back into their casing as before and fired with the strongest possible wood fire; for the hotter the fire is, the whiter the vessels become.

A decorative woodcut tailpiece featuring symmetrical interlaced scrollwork and flourishes.

<column-break/>

# PHILALETHA ILLUSTRATED,
->Or<-
# AN OPEN ENTRANCE
->To the<-
# CLOSED PALACE
->OF THE KING,<-
->Explained with certain new observations.<-
### WITH A NEW PREFACE,
->*In which the life as well as the writings, both published and unpublished, of Eirenaeus Philalethes are briefly set forth.*<-
->To these is added an account<-
->**OF THE LIFE AND WRITINGS OF STARCKIUS,**<-
->As well as<-
->**DR. BECHER’S PANTALEON**<-
->unmasked.<-
->By the efforts of<-
### Joh. Michael Faust,
->Doctor of Medicine and Physician-in-Ordinary of the Republic of Frankfurt am Main, in the College of Curiosities called the "Theophilus."<-

---

->*Frankfurt am Main*, 1728.<-
->Published by J. BENJ. ANDREÆ & HENR. HORT.<-

# TO THE MEN
## Distinguished by the Splendor of their Lineage and Merits,
->Illustrious in Glory,<-

### Magnificent, Active, Most Noble, and Most Worshipful,

# TO THE LORD PRAETOR, <note>The <term>Praetor</term> (or *Schultheiß*) was the highest judicial official in the city, representing the Emperor's authority.</note>
# TO THE LORD CONSULS, <note>The <term>Consuls</term> refers to the Burgomasters or Mayors who headed the city's executive council.</note>
# TO THE LORD SCABINI, <note>The <term>Scabini</term> (or *Schöffen*) were the lay judges or aldermen who sat on the city court.</note>
# TO THE LORD SYNDICS AND SENATORS, <note>The <term>Syndics</term> were legal counsel for the city, while <term>Senators</term> were members of the governing council.</note>

## Of the famous and very ancient Imperial Free City of Frankfurt, <note>original: "Francofurtensis". Frankfurt was an "Imperial Free City," meaning it was a self-governing territory responsible only to the Holy Roman Emperor.</note>
### In the Practice of Affairs and the Prudence of Governing
->Most highly praised,<-

## My Lords and Patrons,
->To be honored in the highest degree,<-
->as well as to be respected,<-

->*Wishes these pages, such as they are, to be dedicated to them*<-
### Johann Michael Faust. <note>Johann Michael Faust (1663–1707) was a physician in Frankfurt and the editor of this collection of alchemical works.</note>

<meta>catchword: tim</meta>

*...of medical use, I have discussed not infrequently and reviewed through inquiry. For I hold it as a proven fact that we can aspire to the highest knowledge of natural things—so far as is possible given our human weakness—by three steps, as it were. These are **Metallurgy** <note>term: Metallurgy (Metallurgia). In this period, metallurgy was not just industrial smelting but the foundational science of identifying and purifying substances from the earth.</note>, which is occupied with properly identifying and separating natural bodies <note>term: natural bodies (corpora naturalia). A general term for any physical substance found in nature, including ores, plants, and animal matter.</note>, and prepares them so conveniently that they can afterwards be more successfully digested <note>In early chemistry, "digestion" (digeri) referred to the process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat to soften or decompose it.</note> and applied to more difficult operations. Indeed, from here immediate-*

*-ly <note>The Latin word "statim" (immediately) is split across the page break; the syllable "-tim" completes the word and serves as the catchword.</note> **Chemistry** <note>term: Chemistry (Chemia). Here distinguished from metallurgy as a secondary stage focused on extraction and medical application.</note> claims the second place for itself. It teaches how to draw out the best juices <note>original: "succos." In early modern science, "juices" referred to the essential liquids, extracts, or medicinal essences found within raw materials.</note> from those separated bodies and aptly convert them into human uses suitable for medical matters. From this it is clear that chemistry encompasses within its scope not only minerals, but also the things growing from the earth itself and other animals, and thus extends most widely. In contrast, **Alchemy** is of a deeper investigation; it explains the hidden method of preparing gold, to which other more noble metals <note>term: noble metals (metalla nobiliora). Typically referring to gold and silver, which were considered "noble" because they do not rust or perish in fire.</note> are also referred. And indeed the former...*

<meta>catchword: sic</meta>

A traditional way of referring to students of medicine or those within the medical profession.</note>; no one, I believe, who considers the matter in the right way, will call this into doubt. But as for **Alchemy** <note>term: Alchemy (Alchemia). The author treats Alchemy as the highest "step" of science, distinct from basic chemistry by its spiritual requirements and its focus on the "most divine" secrets of matter.</note>, a far different account must surely be held. Since it is granted by a singular favor of the Divine Power <note>original: "Numinis beneficio." Referring to the will or grace of God.</note> and lies hidden in secret, it falls to none but those who know how to seek the divine blessing through sanctity of life and conduct and long experience in practical matters. Hence, it can easily be separated from abuse and be restored to its rightful purity. Meanwhile,

# DEDICATION

<sig>b 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: maxi-</meta>

*thus* my reasoning suggests that the golden saying of the Poet must be entirely kept in mind: *Nothing in excess* <note>original: "Ne quid nimis." A famous classical proverb (often attributed to the Seven Sages of Greece and used by poets like Terence) advocating for moderation and caution.</note>. This is both because that most divine of Arts lies hidden in secret, and because *it is not permitted for everyone to go to Corinth* <note>original: "non omnibus Corinthum adire liceat." A classical proverb meaning that not everyone has the means, the talent, or the divine favor to achieve a difficult or expensive goal.</note>, unless one happens to be equipped with solid reasons and the most certain experiments, or by some singular Gift of the Divine Power. By which very fact, however, I by no means make the certainty or the truth of this principal Art ambiguous; for who is so witless or absurd as to wish to call into doubt that which...

<summary>The author identifies Alchemy as the third and most difficult step of natural science, requiring divine favor and moral purity. He cautions that this "divine art" is not accessible to everyone, using classical proverbs to emphasize the need for moderation and specialized skill.</summary>

## Preface to the Reader
## By Johann Michael Faust.

There are many things indeed which drive me to the opinion that I should look a little more accurately into our Philalethes <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes (meaning "peaceful lover of truth") was the pseudonym of a highly influential 17th-century alchemist, widely believed to be the American-born George Starkey, though his identity remained a mystery to many contemporary European scholars like Faust.</note>, whose *Palace* <note>original: "Palatium" — referring to the famous alchemical treatise "Introitus Apertus ad Occlusum Regis Palatium" (An Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King).</note> we are now publishing, enlightened by certain new observations. So far as my busy schedule permits, I shall bring to light a few things concerning his way of life as well as his writings, which have scarcely—if even at all—appeared in Germany. For I do not think we should listen to those who cast doubt on both, and plainly deny that Philalethes ever existed among human affairs...

<summary>Johann Michael Faust concludes his formal dedication to the Frankfurt city leaders and begins a scholarly preface. He aims to defend the historical reality and clarify the biography of the mysterious alchemist Eirenaeus Philalethes, whose "Closed Palace of the King" he is reediting.</summary>

<keywords>Johann Michael Faust, Eirenaeus Philalethes, alchemy, Frankfurt, history of medicine, Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King, 18th-century publishing</keywords>

<meta>catchword: Auto-</meta>

[Phi]lalethes (whether that name be at last a true one or merely a persona), then they even decide that his Tincture <term>Tincture: In alchemy, this refers to the "Philosopher's Stone" or the medicinal elixir capable of transmuting metals and healing the body.</term> should be placed among what they call "beings of reasoning reason" <note>original: "Entia rationis ratiocinantis" — A philosophical term for concepts that exist only in the mind and have no objective reality.</note>. I will not now refute this latter assertion at length, as it easily collapses under its own futility and could never find approval among the leading men of the chemical arts <term>chemical arts: "re Chemica" — here meaning alchemy, the predecessor to modern chemistry</term>, whose illustrious names are read everywhere. 

But as to why their minds are so hesitant regarding the former point [his identity], I have not yet been able to scent out a true and clear cause. For I think they will grant that all those writings circulated under the name of Philalethes acknowledge a single author; the style of writing is clearly the same, as is the method of treatment, and finally the same principles easily prove it. There are, therefore, among the English themselves those who claim to have collected from papers

written by this author’s own hand, that he should be referred to the French rather than the British if you look at his origin. They believe this was done so that he might hide more safely in France, since it is certain that, by some fate, followers of alchemy are treated very poorly in those lands and are almost severely prosecuted. 

However, I myself have seen and heard many men of no low rank in England who strove to claim and assert Eirenaeus for themselves and their country, as being born of English parents but more highly skilled in the French language, in which he composed his most important writings. For those works which Langius and Birrus <note>Joachim Lange and Martinus Birrius were influential editors and translators who helped disseminate alchemical texts in the 17th century.</note> published under that name, elaborated in the Roman tongue <note>original: "Romana dictione" — Latin</note>, are to be regarded entirely as translations. # PREFACE

<header>Preface to the Reader</header>
<meta>catchword: difci-</meta>

...who first translated his little books into the English tongue is a matter more for curious inquiry than for certain or prompt statement. Regarding this *Entrance to the Shut Palace of the King* <note>original: "Introitu hoc ad Occlusum Regis Palatium" — One of the most famous alchemical treatises of the 17th century, attributed to Eirenaeus Philalethes.</note>, it must be judged differently, since the most experienced Author himself polished it with a second care and gave it forth expanded with new observations. Therefore, when I was staying in England, I learned from men of proven faith that our Philalethes (whether that name be genuine or assumed) had a literary correspondence with many Englishmen; whether he did this himself or through others is of no concern to know. It is now a pleasure to say in support of this opinion that the most illustrious Boyle <note>Robert Boyle (1627–1691), a founding father of modern chemistry who was deeply interested in alchemical transmutation.</note> shared letters with him and cultivated a friendship on more than one account. It happened once that this "Hero of the Gown" <note>original: "Togatus iste Heros" — A respectful term for Boyle as a man of learning and noble status.</note> was

<column-break/>

<header>By Joh. Michael Faust</header>
<sig>c 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: Phar-</meta>

by chance visited by a disciple of a certain Adept <term>Adept: In alchemy, a person who has successfully discovered the Philosopher's Stone and mastered the "Great Work" of transmutation.</term> (who was later thrown into chains among the French). When this man, under Boyle's own inspection, had converted Lead into Gold, and was subsequently asked whether he knew Philalethes, he affirmed that he was at that very time hiding in France. Upon receiving this news, [Boyle] handed reply letters to this man so that, given the opportunity, he might deliver them to Philalethes. But while occupied on that very journey, the man was thrown to the ground with a heavy wound by a fall from the relay horse <note>original: "equi dispositi" — A horse from a postal or courier system designed for rapid travel.</note> he was riding and met his final day. Thus, by this unhappy accident, he choked off the hope of delivering the replies to Philalethes. But there are still other things which seem to shed light on this very obscure matter. For it is well known among all who are touched by any care for natural things, that there recently passed away in London...

# PREFACE

<header>Preface to the Reader</header>
<meta>catchword: fet,</meta>

...[There was] a certain apothecary most skilled in the Chemical Art, known by the name of *Starkey* <note>George Starkey (1628–1665) was a colonial American physician and alchemist who is now widely identified as the author behind the "Eirenaeus Philalethes" pseudonym, though Faust treats them here as two distinct people.</note>, a man quite learned and ingenious, as his writings (to be reviewed below) abundantly testify. Concerning this man, there is a consistent rumor—confirmed even by the judgment <note>original: "calculo" — literally 'by the pebble' or 'calculation,' used here to mean a formal opinion or testimony.</note> of the most Illustrious Boyle himself—that he was American by nationality, having been born in New England. It was there that he found the opportunity to enter into a very close friendship with Philalethes. This friendship was so great that Philalethes entered his chemical workshop on more than one occasion and frequently performed the most elegant experiments of his Art; indeed, he even gave Starkey several pieces of gold as a gift. From these gifts, while he was living sumptuously, it happened—as is often the case when envy intervenes—that he became suspected by Philalethes of having intercepted some of his secrets,

<column-break/>

<header>By Joh. Michael Faust</header>
<sig>c 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: inte-</meta>

and of having published them prematurely; consequently, he was finally abandoned by him. This was the reason that moved Starkey to depart for Europe. Thus, having arrived in London, he immediately displayed specimens of his singular chemical expertise, which he either attributed to Philalethes or had achieved himself, guided by other examples. It must also not be overlooked that Starkey published many works which clearly align with the teachings of his Master and are, at the very least, derived from his "fields" <note>original: "ex arvis ejus" — a metaphorical way of saying 'from his body of work' or 'foundations'.</note>. Furthermore, he asserted on more than one occasion both that he had seen Philalethes and that he had cultivated a most devoted friendship with him. However, he was unfortunate in this one respect: because he lived quite extravagantly and neglected his private affairs, he suffered a heavy loss of his wealth and thus (as they say) came to a miserable end. # PREFACE

<header>Preface to the Reader</header>
<meta>catchword: kio,</meta>

Meanwhile, it is certain that if the Booksellers <note>original: "Bibliopolæ"</note> who printed the Works of Philalethes were willing to confess the truth about where they received those booklets from, or in what language <note>original: "idiomate"</note> they were written, that dispute could easily be settled. In particular, **William Cooper**, a London Bookseller, must be named by me here publicly, as he saw to it that many works by this Author were printed. But even *Lang* and *Birrius* <note>Likely Johann Lange and Martin Birrius, contemporary editors and translators of alchemical texts into Latin.</note> were not ignorant of where they received those manuscripts, or under what name they were delivered to them. Though it must also be warned here that many works have long been suspected by the Learned, which are nevertheless commonly attributed to Philalethes—such as, perhaps, the *Exposition on Flamel, Artephius, and Sendivogius* <note>These are three legendary alchemists; Flamel is the famous French scrivener, Artephius a medieval authority, and Sendivogius a Polish adept.</note>, regarding which there will be a place to provide warning elsewhere. It will now be helpful instead to provide a List of Books published both by Starkey,

<sig>c 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: 1. In-</meta>

...key <note>This continues the name "Starkey" from the previous column.</note> (whether he was ultimately a servant or a companion and friend of our Eirenaeus), as well as by Philalethes himself. It is a matter of regret, however, that many of these works rarely reach our hands, and many are written only in the English language. To be sure, Starkey primarily focused on these: *The Vindication of Helmont* <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a pioneer of pneumatic chemistry whom Starkey greatly admired.</note> and *Pyrotechny*, both composed in the English tongue. But more of Philalethes’ works have come into our hands, and we trust that taking an account of them here will be neither unpleasant nor burdensome for you, Kind Reader. 13. *An Exposition on Flamel, Artephius, and Sendivogius*: whether Philalethes actually wrote this, however, others doubt.

<header>Preface to the Reader. Georg Wolfgang Wedel.</header>
<meta>catchword: Quod</meta>

->In the Name of Jesus<-

# TO THE BENEVOLENT READER
# GEORG WOLFGANG WEDEL.

What the Divine Old Man <note>Wedel refers to Hippocrates, the ancient Greek physician</note> set forth at the beginning of his aphorisms—"experience is deceptive, and the experiment is difficult" <note>original Greek: ἡ δὲ πεῖρα σφαλερὴ (hē de peira sphalerē)</note>—fits the Divine Art, Alchemy, first and foremost. It applies so strongly that two groups of men can be distinguished in regard to it: **The Shuddering and the Suffering.**

*The greater part of people recoils in horror, because the footprints of others terrify them. The Art has acquired haters who are both ignorant and incredulous; indeed, they are even its attackers. Let one man stand as an example for all: Thomas Erastus, a very famous Doctor at the nurturing University of Heidelberg in the last century, who took it upon himself to refute Paracelsian dogmas <note>The teachings of Paracelsus (1493–1541), who championed the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine</note> in a massive, laborious volume. Yet he did so with almost no experimentation, relying solely on speculations and preconceived hypotheses. Even a single experience would demonstrate how empty his arguments are, since one must philosophize from actual things and nature; if this is true anywhere, it is certainly in this Science, where Chemistry <note>original: Chimia</note> was famous long before Paracelsus.*

<column-break/>

<meta>catchword: Cini-</meta>

*But if we investigate the causes of this aversion, hatred, and slander, three can be highlighted above all: the Obscurity of the Authors, the Deceitfulness of the Meddlers <note>original: Ardelionum, referring to busy-bodies or fraudulent practitioners</note> who claim to be Chemists, and the Poverty of the Experimenters. It is true that one reads nothing but Riddles, Enigmas, and "Sweet Fables," as it were, if one approaches the texts unprepared. It seems like a kind of labyrinth from which it is scarcely possible to escape once you have entered; the more books you unroll, the more uncertain you become—and yet, all authors amicably agree on the essential points.*

*Fraud occurs nowhere more frequently than in these Labors. Since those who believe easily are easily deceived, and because people feel an indubitable hope for that which they desperately desire, this has provided an opportunity for many deceivers to claim they have mastered the Art. They bait the Wealthy, the Great, and others from whom they hope to hunt for profit. It may be that they deceive themselves first, and not all are malicious makers of tricks; they might truly believe they are Adepts <note>An "Adept" is a chemist who has successfully discovered the Philosophers' Stone</note> and imagine mountains of gold while lacking the funds for daily life. Yet in the end, their situation often leads to the rope <note>A common historical metaphor for suicide or total ruin</note>, which is why the Chemist is commonly regarded as a deceiver.*

*Finally, Poverty and the loss of great expenses await most seekers who, gasping for the prize or believing themselves or others, spare no cost and hire laborers...*

<summary>This preface by Georg Wolfgang Wedel introduces the study of alchemy by addressing its controversial reputation, blaming its bad name on obscure writing, fraudulent "gold-makers," and the financial ruin of unprepared seekers.</summary>
<keywords>Georg Wolfgang Wedel, Hippocrates, Thomas Erastus, Paracelsus, alchemy vs. chemistry, scientific method, fraud in alchemy, Adepts, chemical experiments</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by Georg Wolfgang Wedel. On the previous page, Wedel introduced the difficulties of alchemy and defended the "Divine Art" against critics like Thomas Erastus, arguing that the failures of some do not invalidate the science itself.</meta>

<header>Preface to the Reader by Georg Wolfgang Wedel.</header>
<meta>catchword: Ciniflones</meta>

**Puffers** <note>original: "Ciniflones," literally "ash-blowers," a derogatory term for amateur alchemists who spent all their time at the bellows</note>, men of every sort, build furnaces, gather a great heap of minerals, and—to obtain the stone—they leave no stone unturned, day and night, until they bring home an empty purse at their own loss. They lament their lost riches, and eventually, those from the common folk devote themselves to base trades, hardly to be freed from that sweet persuasion of making gold. For they carry these gold-making ideas so firmly impressed upon their brains that often they cannot be erased or wiped away even by a sponge or lye. You would sooner bring a melancholic madman, a raging maniac, or any other person suffering from brain fever back to the right path than you would these brainless **boilers** <note>original: "decoctores," a Latin pun meaning both those who boil substances and those who go bankrupt</note>.

Yet Alchemy has not ceased to be cultivated, nor has it deserved to be rejected, condemned, or outlawed. **Abuse does not take away the use**; otherwise, honor would have to be stripped from Astrology and Philosophy, and indeed from the remaining highest peaks of divine and human knowledge. One must philosophize soberly in this study, which is the most ancient, most noble, and most useful.

Those who try to deny its antiquity strive in vain, a fact which Reinesius <note>Thomas Reinesius (1587–1667), a German physician and philologist</note> and Borrichius <note>Olaus Borrichius (1626–1690), a Danish polymath who wrote extensively on the history of alchemy</note> especially have sufficiently proven. And here, besides other supreme gifts which he shared with himself and others, he had obtained a manuscript codex of ancient Greek chemists from Morhof <note>Daniel Georg Morhof (1639–1691)</note>; this was indeed from the Court Library of Gotha of the late **FREDERICK**, the most wise Duke of Saxony, our most merciful Lord, along with

<column-break/>

a most learned preface, written in the native language <note>German</note> by Reinesius; indeed, it contained his polished corrections throughout. It seems that on this occasion, Reinesius especially wrote his *Conspectus of Illustrious Chemical Writers*. He reviews those Greek authors there as well as the others, introducing a general overview through knowledge of the authors, along with excellent advice. I call to witness the man himself, who on pages 9, 8, and 13 cites Reinesius, whom it was necessary to read; and those who remain in that most praised Court are aware with me of this history.

Nor are these all the writers; indeed, Borel <note>Pierre Borel (c. 1620–1671), who published the first major bibliography of alchemy</note> attempted to list them in an entire library, though even he did not know or see them all. Since we are only skimming the matter summarily at this time, we pass over these things and touch upon the **Nobility** of the art in a single word. For it leads to the secrets of Nature, and to the admiration of Divine Majesty, and to genuine wisdom. Far be it from us, however, to approve of what some chemists distort here; carried away by their own fantasies, they either turn Sacred Scripture into their own allegories (from which even our Philalethes did not entirely abstain), or they apply the principles of matter—**Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury**—to the very mysteries of the Divinity, where nothing material has a place or should even be conceived.

But what shall we say of its **Utility**? True chemists are content with the knowledge of the Art, and not for one reason alone...

<meta>catchword: only</meta>

<summary>Wedel distinguishes between "puffers" (foolish amateurs who bankrupt themselves) and true alchemical philosophers. He defends the antiquity and nobility of alchemy, citing historical scholars and manuscripts, while warning against those who mix material chemistry with religious dogma.</summary>
<keywords>puffers, ash-blowers, Reinesius, Borrichius, Duke of Saxony, Gotha Library, Salt-Sulphur-Mercury, chemical bibliography, Eirenaeus Philalethes</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by Georg Wolfgang Wedel. On the previous page, Wedel discussed the "puffers" and "boilers"—those who waste their fortunes on false alchemy—contrasting them with true philosophers who seek the art for divine glory rather than greed.</meta>

# Preface to the Reader

...under this name, they derive its use for the benefit of the human race, setting sail toward the divine glory by healing and helping through this Art, while indeed detesting greed, luxury, and every form of abuse.

Nor have there been lacking in our own century those who devoted themselves to this study, praying devoutly in silence and hope, searching soberly, and working circumspectly without those massive expenses. They followed the **Royal Road** <note>original: "Viam Regiam," a common alchemical term for the direct, "shorter" path to the Philosopher's Stone</note> of the Ancients, cutting away long-winded detours, without the pomp of furnaces or the vain efforts of oversized flasks. This age does not yield to any past eras, whether you consider the brilliant experiments performed or the books published, and the most splendid advancement gained for the Art on both sides.

Well known is the **Projection** <note>term: Projection refers to the final stage of the alchemical process, where a small amount of the Philosopher's Stone is "projected" or cast into a molten base metal to transform it into gold or silver</note> performed by Alexander Seton, an Englishman, in Basel in the year 1603. In the presence of Johann Wolfgang Dienheim and Jakob Zwinger, he converted lead into the finest gold. The eyewitness Dienheim describes this more fully in his *Universal Medicine* <note>original: "Medicin. Univers."</note>, chapter 24, page 64 and following; and Theodor Zwinger asserts in his *Very Curious Scrutiny of the Magnet* <note>original: "Scrutinio Magnetis Curiosissimo"</note>, chapter 3, page 20, that he still preserves a portion of that very gold inherited from his great-grandfather.

Jean-Baptiste Morin <note>A famous French mathematician and astrologer (1583–1656)</note> reports in the preface to his *Gallic Astrology* <note>original: "Astrol. Gallic."</note>, page 1, that he saw gold made from lead through projection that was far better than natural gold. In the testing process, to the great astonishment of the examiner, it even converted a significant portion of additional lead into gold, due to the excess of **Tincture** <note>term: Tincture refers to the concentrated "medicine" or powder of the Philosopher's Stone capable of "dyeing" or transforming metals</note> absorbed by the projected powder.

<column-break/>

### By Georg Wolfgang Wedel.
### d
<meta>catchword: in</meta>

What was achieved in Prague before the Divine Emperor **Ferdinand III** <note>Holy Roman Emperor (reigned 1637–1657), who was a known patron of alchemy</note> deserves to be read in Zwelfer’s *Spagyric Appendix* <note>original: "Mantiss. Spagyr."</note>, part 1, chapter 1, page 796. The Emperor, with his own hand, "tinted" three pounds of mercury into two and a half pounds of pure gold by the benefit of a single grain of the **Philosophers' Tincture**. The same was performed in Mainz in 1658 by the "Lord of Chaos" <note>This refers to Johann Joachim Becher or his associate, the alchemist Richthausen, who used the title "Baron Chaos"</note>, the author of the Tincture, before the Elector of Mainz, Johann Philipp. We ourselves possess a small fragment of this gold, which was passed down from the late Johann Daniel Horst, the supreme chief physician, to his son Johann Otto Horst, the heir of his father’s virtues and glory.

Johann Friedrich Helvetius himself abundantly describes the transmutation performed at his own home in The Hague in his book *The Golden Calf* <note>original: "Vitulo Aureo," a famous 1667 account of an alchemist receiving the Stone from a stranger</note>, where we once saw the remaining traces of the gold. We pass over other examples described here and there, or those that are already well known.

As far as published writings are concerned, those deserve praise which have come from true possessors of the secret, not those cobbled together from other books or fashioned from empty imagination. We will touch upon at least two, praised by our author: Michael Sendivogius's *New Chemical Light* <note>original: "Novum Lumen Chimicum"</note> and Jean d’Espagnet's *The Secret Work of Hermetic Philosophy* <note>original: "Arcanum Philosophiæ Hermeticæ Opus"</note>. For he took these as models to follow; indeed, he explained them more fully and progressed even further.

We will not inquire now whether that Sendivogius is the same person as the aforementioned Alexander Seton, or if he was his...

<summary>Wedel lists historical testimonies and eyewitness accounts of successful alchemical transmutations, citing specific authors, emperors, and physicians as evidence for the reality of the Art. He highlights the work of Seton, Helvetius, and Sendivogius as reliable sources for students of alchemy.</summary>
<keywords>transmutation, projection, Alexander Seton, Ferdinand III, Tincture, gold-making, Johann Friedrich Helvetius, Sendivogius, Jean d’Espagnet, historical alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion of Alexander Seton's legacy and the historical debate surrounding the death of the famous alchemist. Wedel is moving toward a comparison of the most reliable alchemical authors of the 17th century.</meta>

...sufficient as a successor in marriage, [it is said] that Oswald Croll saw the **Philosopher’s Stone** <note>original: "Lapidem Philosophorum"</note> in his possession, as Croll testifies in the preface of the *Royal Chemical House* <note>original: "Basilica Chimica," a famous textbook of medical alchemy</note>, which he wrote in the year 1608. At that time, Croll mentions him as already dead; yet Dienheim, writing in 1610, asserts that Seton was still living.

Let Michael Sendivogius be considered the noble one, Jean d'Espagnet the clear-sighted one, and our Philalethes the experienced one—if he is not to be preferred above the rest, then he is at least worthy to be joined as the third in this complete **Trio** <note>original: "Triga," literally a three-horse chariot, used here to mean a prestigious group of three</note> of the best writers. This *Entrance* <note>referring to Eirenaeus Philalethes’ work, "Introitus Apertus ad Occlusum Regis Palatium" (An Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King)</note> was received with public applause, and from that point, work began not only to follow its model but also to refine and establish the "subject" <note>In alchemy, the "subject" is the raw material used at the beginning of the Great Work</note> more sublimely, which the text was commonly thought to suggest.

To deny nothing, since that time **Antimony** <note>A brittle, silvery-white metalloid central to many alchemical processes</note> has more greatly favored the lovers of chemistry, as well as its **Martial Regulus** <note>term: A "regulus" is a metallic button found at the bottom of a crucible; "Martial" means it was purified using iron (associated with the god Mars)</note>. This was to such an extent that Johann Joachim Becher with singular zeal in more than one place, the "Suburban Hermit" in his *Chemical Aphorisms*, and many others have striven to elevate it to the throne of the **Sophic Secret** <note>original: "Arcani Sophici," the secret of the wise</note>, indeed holding it as the only valid path. Today it is almost considered a paradox if anyone dares to deny it. From this, one may borrow a key, as it were—if one wishes to follow this path—from two other authors, namely Alexander von Suchten (I wonder if that Alexander Seton wished to be called by this name, as he claimed it?), in his *Treatise on Antimony* <note>original: "Tract. de Antimonio"</note>, page 299, and Zwelfer in the cited place.

<column-break/>

# Georg Wolfgang Wedel.

<meta>catchword: unus</meta>

In the *Spagyric Supplement* <note>original: "Mantiss. Spagir."</note>, part 2, chapter 7, page 828. It is not our purpose to pursue these matters more extensively, since we have set our mind to write a Preface, not a Commentary.

Let us leave these labors and seemingly probable reasons to them; nevertheless, we cannot help but raise at least certain doubts. Indeed, from a careful reading of Suchten himself, one can catch a scent that his own mind was elsewhere; and whoever reads the treatises of Pantaleon—that excellent follower and, as it were, commentator on Philalethes—will unwillingly confess the same. It must be assumed, however, that these "Philosophers by Fire" write in their own manner, and do not wish to be understood literally. Indeed, while they solicitously inform the reader of their purpose, they do not cease to be honest even while insinuating other things, cunningly hiding what they truly intend.

Let Pantaleon himself be a witness, who, if read more deeply, impresses upon us something other than Antimony by a very clear example, and a culinary one at that; indeed, he rejects minerals and metals except for two: the Fiery and the Watery. Nay, if the words of Philalethes are weighed more attentively, and you know the **Hieroglyphics** <note>referring to the symbolic and emblematic language used by alchemists to hide their meanings</note> of the ancients, the contrary can easily be revealed; if, for example, we knew what the **Offspring of Saturn** <note>original: "Saturni soboles." In alchemical riddles, Saturn is lead; his "offspring" is often interpreted as antimony, which looks like lead but has different properties</note> is, and why Alexander the Great and many other Greek kings wished to be seen conspicuously with **Aries' horns** <note>original: "Cornibus Arietinis." A reference to the Ram, the zodiac sign of Spring, often signaling the time to begin the alchemical work</note> even on coins, to say nothing of other matters.

If other writers are joined to these, while that which...

<summary>Wedel discusses the credibility of famous alchemists like Seton and Philalethes, noting the popular but potentially misleading focus on antimony as the primary material for the Philosopher's Stone. He warns the reader that these authors use symbolic "hieroglyphic" language and culinary metaphors to hide their true methods.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues Wedel’s introductory defense of the alchemist Philalethes. Having established the author’s prestige alongside d'Espagnet and Sendivogius, Wedel now addresses the difficulties of the text and the skepticism of other scholars, specifically Georg Horn.</meta>

# Preface to the Reader

One is silent, the other names things more clearly; one must be explained by the other, so that errors can be avoided—errors into which beginners most easily fall, and even the Veteran Masters of the Art. Thus, it seems he took the **Doves of Diana** <note>original: "Columbas Dianæ"; an alchemical symbol for the "spirit" or the white sublimate that appears during the purification of metals</note> from d'Espagnet, who best teaches the attentive reader what they are, although it is written by others, and by Borrichius himself, that another nearer path is given besides these.

We wished to touch on these things at least in passing, so as not to invite the Reader entirely without some warning. Nor should it be omitted that, for the cure of diseases, medicines prepared with **Antimony** <note>A brittle, metallic substance central to 17th-century medical chemistry</note> according to that measure were sufficiently excellent; but for the **Tincture** <note>The legendary "Philosopher's Stone" used to transform base metals into gold</note>, nothing has been seen to be perfect in this way, nothing <term>worthy of mention <note>original: "ἄξιον λόγου" (axion logou)</note></term>, up to this day. Hence they seem to have wanted to show the true way by such an ambiguous example, which should also be said of **Basil Valentine** <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist, though his works were likely written in the early 1600s</note> and others.

From the number of those who labored in vain in this way, we cannot help but praise Georg Horn—a most distinguished man in other respects and nurtured in these studies—who, in his Preface to the *Chemistry of Geber the Arab*, wrote thus of our author: *"There appeared lately,"* he says, *"the anonymous Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King, sent to me many years ago as a manuscript from England; a work entirely Sophistic and Fictitious, which by experience was known to many who tested it exactly, with the loss*

<column-break/>

### Georg Wolfgang Wedel
<sig>d 3</sig>

loss of expense and time."

It pleased us, indeed for more than one reason, to recommend a new edition of this small but **Golden Treatise** from the vast abundance of such writings. For it matters not a little which Master or Author you choose in this Genre. Indeed, it is to be wished that Physicians, and especially those in Academies, and even other learned men, would take up this labor, or even dedicate some leisure hours to the reading of these Chemical Authors—at least so that they are not condemned unheard, which is very harsh.

The same study is fitting for Theologians, Jurists <note>original: "Ictos," an abbreviation for Jurisconsultos</note>, Historians, and all literary men. Everyone will find there something to judge and marvel at, something to delight in and profit from. Nor does it stand in the way that the Author concealed his name, who is nevertheless commonly held to be a native of England (a most cultured nation which already some centuries ago had **Adept Chemists** <note>Those who have successfully mastered the "Great Work" of alchemy</note>, and possesses them today without doubt), and is called **Thomas de Vagan** <note>Thomas Vaughan (1621–1666), a famous Welsh alchemist and mystic frequently identified as the true author of Philalethes’ works</note>.

He did not wish, at least while living, to become known, lest trouble be created for him; yet he could not remain so hidden that he was not made known to the Curious. However, we cannot report more, except that his other writings are also celebrated, concerning which—as well as more things worthy of knowing about our Author, indeed about...

<summary>Wedel addresses the criticisms of George Horn, who called the 'Open Entrance' a fraud, and argues that the book is actually a "golden treatise" worthy of study by all scholars. He also identifies the anonymous author as the Englishman Thomas Vaughan (Thomas de Vagan).</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues Wedel’s introductory defense of the alchemist Philalethes. # Preface to the Reader
<sig>b</sig>

Regarding the *Universal Art*, one can read in Morhof’s <note>Daniel Georg Morhof (1639–1691), a prominent German scholar who wrote extensively on the history of alchemy</note> most elegant book *On the Transmutation of Metals*, page 143 and following, where his sincere conclusion is: *“it appears that no one has dealt with this subject more candidly and with greater clarity than he.”* And this very *Entrance* <note>Referring to the title of the work: *Introitus Apertus ad Occlusum Regis Palatium* (An Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King)</note> can suffice for the present; the rest should be regarded as rivers, flowing more widely from that source, yet not to be neglected.

Furthermore, the Author maintains the order of treatment he set for himself; yet he pursues his web so distinctly and expertly—if I may put it that way—that even if his assertions are placed separately and reduced into the form of a process, he describes the same <term>operation <note>original Greek: "εγχειρησιν" (encheirēsin); refers to the manual labor or practical procedure of alchemy</note></term> across many chapters. If we recognize this, the concept can become more evident and the truth can be drawn out, unlike if little flowers <note>original: "floſculi"; a common metaphor for rhetorical flourishes or isolated excerpts</note> had to be plucked here and there from a more diffuse mass. He conducts himself in such a way that, as he progresses, he explains more clearly those things that were mentioned more briefly in earlier parts.

He also shows himself to be somewhat timid in making this treasure public, fearing for his life—and not without cause. For although this might be believed less necessary in populous cities, examples teach us that trust is never safe. Thomas Norton, the Englishman, mentions the dangers faced by Thomas Dalton <note>Norton’s *Ordinal of Alchemy* tells of Dalton, a monk supposedly persecuted and imprisoned by those seeking the secret of the Stone</note> (in his *Chemical Treatise*, chapter 2), and other examples are well-known and touched upon here and there. Owners of the secret consider themselves safer under 

<column-break/>

**Georg Wolfgang Wedel’s [Preface]**
<sig>d 4</sig>

contempt, so that the Art itself is also more effectively hidden by this very means.

Regarding things that might finally seem in need of change in this or that word, we have preferred to leave most as they were; in writings of this kind, the subject matter must be weighed more than the specific words or style. We have followed the edition published by **Johannes Lange** in *Amsterdam, 1667*, whose Preface we also subjoin. This same text can be found printed in the *Enlarged Hermetic Museum*, Title XVI. Farewell, Reader, and hold Philalethes as worth all others; consider this *Entrance* a Compendium of Universal Alchemy, where you will find a few <term>exoteric <note>Public or outer teachings meant for the uninitiated</note></term> things, but many <term>acroamatic <note>Secret or inner teachings intended only for those advanced enough to hear them</note></term> ones.

->Given from my study at Jena, 1698.<-
->September 21st.<-

# PREFACE BY THE FIRST EDITOR, JOHANN LANGE, TO THE READER.

When, not so long ago, I obtained this little Work (which I here give to the public) from a certain most excellent Man, highly skilled in these matters, *Benevolent Reader*, it immediately pleased me so much that I took the counsel to share it for your benefit right then and there. I was driven to this because the Author seemed to be nearly the only genuine worker and possessor of the <term>Physical Tincture <note>Another name for the Philosopher's Stone or the universal medicine, capable of purifying both metals and the human body.</note></term> in this century in which we live. He composed his writing with the intention that, once published, an <term>Adept <note>A term for a chemist who has successfully achieved the "Great Work" of alchemy.</note></term> might be recognized, and those wandering aimlessly in the Art might be brought back to the right path. I was also moved by the candor and <term>clarity <note>original Greek: "σαφήνεια" (saphēneia); refers to the perspicuity or transparency of the writing.</note></term> of the text itself, and by its continuous thread which follows the style of <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist whose works were considered the gold standard for clarity in the 17th century.</note> Sendivogius. The Author follows in his footsteps everywhere, often even surpassing him, and always explains matters exceptionally well, even without being asked. I trusted, therefore, that by publishing this, the most praiseworthy

<column-break/>

<header>Preface to the Reader by Johann Lange.</header>
<sig>d 3</sig>

intention of the Author would be satisfied, and a light might be kindled in the moonless night of errors for the many who even today plot to obtain the great <term>Philosopher's Stone</term> through either painful labor, arrogant science, or wicked sighs—even though, alas! their hopes are repeatedly cut short. For although a few years ago many laboriously sought the title of "True Philosophy" and presumed to teach—or rather, I should say, to drive mad—the world with fantastic booklets under the mask of a false name, they even dragged the ignorance of certain noteworthy men onto the stage, much to the laughter of the learned. However, sharper minds and those who (as the saying goes) were more familiarly acquainted with the "dead Philosophers" <note>A reference to studying the classic, ancient texts of alchemy rather than modern pamphlets.</note> immediately detected their frauds and "hooked" tricks. Thus, these <term>worthless <note>original Latin: "hordeatici" (literally "made of barley"). In Roman times, barley was the food of the poor or a punishment for soldiers; here it means cheap or fraudulent.</note></term> promisers, swelling with such a wide gap between their words and reality, were soon after abandoned by their admirers—much like the donkey who wore the lion's skin—and have since paid the <term>penalty for their stupidity <note>original: "blacenuomion," a Latinized Greek construction for the price or custom of being a fool.</note></term> among the forgers. 

But this man, whom we now present for the common benefit, preserves the style and thread of the noble Philosophers everywhere; he breathes primordial Chemistry in every line and reveals himself abundantly through his own signs. Thus, I hope it will happen that, with this radiance having arisen, the falsifications and "patchwork" theories of others—

<summary>Johann Lange introduces his edition of the text, arguing that the author is a true Adept who follows the clear style of Sendivogius. He contrasts this genuine work with the fraudulent and "fantastic" alchemical booklets that were common at the time.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Lange, Physical Tincture, Adept, Michael Sendivogius, Philosopher's Stone, alchemical fraud, primordial Chemistry</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues Johann Lange's preface. He has just finished praising the clarity and importance of the author’s (Eirenaeus Philalethes) work and is now discussing the mystery of the author's identity and his remarkable achievements at a young age.</meta>

### Preface to the Reader.
<b>b</b>
<meta>catchword: right</meta>

...monies might, like a cloud and smoke, at last be thoroughly dissipated. Truly, who or what sort of man the Author of this <term>fresh little work <note>original Latin: "mustulentæ Opellæ"; literally "a little work like new wine (must)," suggesting it is fresh and potent.</note></term> might be, I know as little as the most ignorant; nor, since He himself wished his name to remain hidden, have I judged it right to inquire against his wishes. It is clear enough, however, that he is a man beloved by God, like a certain Daniel, and truly a <term>man of desires <note>original Latin: "verumque desideriorum Virum"; a biblical reference to Daniel 9:23, implying a man favored by God and filled with holy longing for wisdom.</note></term> (provided he still survives and breathes the vital air). He is one who, at that age, attained the Medical, Chemical, and Physical Secrets of the Philosophers, which many others are barely permitted to know at their peak, and even then only as if through a <term>lattice window <note>original Latin: "per transennam"; meaning to see something only partially or in passing.</note></term>. Certainly, one who has extracted this Art from books at such a tender age will be found to be most rare. Thomas Norton, an Englishman, professed to have known the **White Tincture** in the twenty-eighth year of his age; Van Helmont records how, around the same year, Theophrastus Paracelsus received the **Gold-making Stone**; so too Dionysius Zacharias, and perhaps a few others, are said to have gained possession as young men. But these received it from Teachers, <term>by another's wisdom <note>original Latin: "aliena Minerva"; a proverbial expression meaning through the guidance or inspiration of someone else.</note></term>; which is not so much to be wondered at, since it is merely their capable intellect that deserves praise. But to be able to gather roses from the most thorny thistles and thickets of writers at the twenty-third (or thirty-third) year of age, without a Teacher and by one's own guidance—and

<column-break/>

### Of Johann Lange
<meta>catchword: wrote</term>

rightly to prepare the true **Philosopher’s Stone**—this has perhaps been granted to no one, or as has been said, to the very fewest until now. Who would deny that a Science hidden by GOD was poured down upon such a mind in open <term>showers <note>original Latin: "peroledis"; likely a variant or typo for "perpluviis," implying an abundant raining down of knowledge.</note></term>? For I believe myriads could be counted who, having spent their lives in this study and race-course even unto a great old age, have not been able to greet even the first thresholds of this Wisdom, though not a few have toiled for fifty or sixty years, more or less, in every conceivable labor. All the more should we venerate the <term>great <note>original Greek: "τὸν πάνυ" (ton panyu); an honorific phrase meaning "the altogether great" or "the famous."</note></term> Author, because—though largely overwhelmed by such great and unusual gifts from GOD—he did not, like the <term>Aspendian Lyre-player <note>A proverbial figure who "played for himself inside," used to describe those who keep their talents or secrets to themselves.</note></term>, choose to sing for himself alone. Instead, what he had learned without envy, he wished to communicate kindly without envy. There is no need to recount here how much envy has prevailed among most others in handing down these Secrets, and how they have surrounded their writings with malice, as if by a partition wall; any of the Chemical Seekers can sufficiently attest to this. Indeed, the <term>sons of the Philosophers <note>original Greek: "οἱ τῶν Φιλοσόφων παῖδες" (hoi tōn Philosophōn paides); a traditional way of referring to students or practitioners of alchemy.</note></term> have considered it a point of pride to thus play with posterity, and they have triumphed, as it were, under cover of envy and <term>imaginary cataracts <note>original Latin: "Chimæricisque glaucomatis"; "glaucoma" here refers to a blinding of the eyes or obscuring of the truth.</note></term>. Thus, as is held in Scaliger’s Greek Eusebius, Democritus of Abdera <term>wrote <note>original Greek: "συνέγραψε" (synegrapse); meaning he composed or compiled these accounts.</note></term>...

<summary>Johann Lange discusses the anonymous author's extraordinary achievement of discovering the Philosopher's Stone at a young age without a teacher. He contrasts the author's generosity in sharing his secrets with the traditional secrecy and envy of other alchemists.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues Johann Lange's preface to the reader. Having discussed the youthful brilliance of the anonymous author (Eirenaeus Philalethes), Lange now compares this author's clarity to the legendary obscurity of ancient alchemists like Maria the Jewess and modern masters like Sendivogius, while lamenting the state of the manuscript he is editing.</meta>

### Preface to the Reader by Johann Lange.

[Someone] wrote about gold, silver, stones, and the description of purple. Likewise, Maria <note>original Greek: "Μαρία (τὶς Ἑβραία σοφὴς)"; referring to Mary the Prophetess, a legendary foundational figure in alchemy.</note> (a certain wise Hebrew woman) was praised by the heavens for hiding the Art behind many wise riddles. Pammenes, however, condemned those who wrote without envy <note>The Greek term implies writing "without holding back" or too openly, which was often seen as a violation of alchemical secrecy.</note>. But our author, taught by a better school, has separated the **Chemical Art** from all common errors; and, having shattered the false arguments and exploded the meddlesome dreams of self-styled thinkers, he describes his matters so candidly, clearly, and transparently that you will find so many things more clearly in no one else—as Truth <note>original: "Alerhea," likely a typo for the Greek "Aletheia" (Truth).</note> herself would say. Indeed, if the things which **Mary Rante** <note>Mary Rant, an Englishwoman who published prophecies in the 1650s.</note>, an Englishwoman, promised in a revelatory way concerning the **Making of Gold** <note>original: "Aurifactura."</note>—namely, that it would become common in the year 1661—should finally prove true even after the passing of five years, I would not doubt that one could take heart from it. He follows in the footsteps of **Sendivogius** <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a world-famous Polish alchemist known for his work on "central nitre."</note>, but with a better order and far more accurate distinctions. Granted, concerning the true subject of the Philosophers, he may have taught more obscurely than Sendivogius (I say "more obscurely" in view of beginners, who might draw more easily from Sendivogius); yet certainly, to one looking carefully and without the bias of a preconceived opinion, a Sun more lucid than that of Sendivogius cannot fail to arise. In other matters, neither Sendivogius nor anyone else would dare prefer himself—

<column-break/>

—anyone else, whether of the ancients or of later ages! O, how separate are the streams of Marah and Siloam! <note>A biblical contrast between the "bitter" waters of Marah and the "healing" waters of Siloam.</note> How great is the disparity between these and other writings, which until now have laid paths to precipices for a blind world! Truly, I wish it had pleased the excellent Author to commit his most polished writings to the public press himself—for I hear he has also finished a Dialogue on the liquor of **Water-Fire** or the **Alcahest** <note>The "Alcahest" was the legendary universal solvent of the alchemists, capable of reducing any body to its first matter.</note>—as I have no doubt we would have everything more correctly and perfectly. Now, although I have paid the most scrupulous attention, because it happened that I used a manuscript that was not altogether correct (as is commonly the case), I would not dare to assert that everything fits the Author's words most rigidly. For quite often, because the sense was obviously lacking, it had to be helped by the change of a letter or two. Nevertheless, we have studiously taken care that wherever a consistent sense could be drawn out, we would not disturb anything rashly. We vow, and if indeed the divine Author still lives, we pray and entreat that he might wish to commit his writings to the press himself, corrected and smoothed to his own mind; or if he does not disdain to use us as <term>subordinates <note>original Latin: "mediastinis"; literally domestic servants or helpers of low rank.</note></term>, to bring it about that a genuine manuscript might reach our hands in some way. We would consider it a point of pride to have corrected our work, regarding nothing before the public good...

<meta>catchword: nothing</meta>

<summary>Johann Lange praises the clarity of Eirenaeus Philalethes' alchemical writings, comparing them favorably to the works of Sendivogius and Mary the Prophetess. He also notes the difficulties he faced in editing the text from a flawed manuscript and expresses hope that the anonymous author will eventually release an authorized version.</summary>

<keywords>Johann Lange, Eirenaeus Philalethes, Sendivogius, Maria the Jewess, Mary Rant, Alcahest, Water-Fire, alchemy, chemical art, Marah and Siloam, translation errors</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page concludes the editor Johann Lange's preface to the reader. Lange finishes his justification for publishing the manuscript of Eirenaeus Philalethes, expressing his fear that private copies would become corrupted over time. He then provides the Table of Contents for the work.</meta>

...having nowhere and at no time any greater concern than this, you, *Benevolent Reader*, will interpret these things in the best possible light. Consider that if these matters—like secret encyclopedic knowledge <note>original Greek: "ἐγκύκλια" (enkyklia)</note> and traditions handed down from the fathers <note>original Greek: "πατροπαράδοτα" (patroparadota)</note>—had been forced to continue circulating through clandestine distributions of handwritten copies, perhaps soon even more faults and greater gaps would have crept in, or the true meaning of the Writing itself would have been gradually obliterated. I do not need to explain here at length how this has happened to so many treatises of the highest reputation.

I do not wish to add more here, for I am not attempting to write a Commentary. These things at least seemed worth advising, if only for my own sake: so that the Author himself might not believe I have attempted to alter his own Writing (as the "Straight Sun" <note>original Greek: "ὀρθὸς ἥλιος" (orthos helios); likely a reference to a specific critic or a pseudonym used in the alchemical debates of the time.</note> once presumed to do against Sendivogius <note>Michael Sendivogius, a famous Polish alchemist whose works were often edited or "corrected" by others.</note>), nor that others might judge me unfavorably, as the world is now full of pseudo-intellectuals <note>original: "sciolorum"; those with a superficial or "smattering" of knowledge.</note>. Whatever the case, I wanted to serve the chemical art in this matter and to set sail to meet the desires of many. If I see that this has been welcome, perhaps more will follow; if not—but I reject such a bad omen. Greetings, *Benevolent Reader*, and Farewell. 

Hamburg, on the Ides of December <note>December 13th.</note> in the year 1666.

# AN OPEN ENTRANCE TO THE CLOSED PALACE OF THE KING.

### THE AUTHOR'S PREFACE.

I, having achieved the status of an Adept <note>An "Adept" is someone who has successfully discovered the secret of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, an anonymous philosopher known as Philalethes <note>original: "Philalethe"; a pseudonym meaning "Lover of Truth." He is now widely identified as George Starkey.</note>, in the year 1645 of the world’s redemption, and in the thirty-third year of my age, have decided to write this little treatise. I do so to pay my debt to the sons of the art <note>A traditional term for students and practitioners of alchemy.</note> and to extend a hand to those entangled in the labyrinth of error. My goal is that it may be clear to other Adepts that I am their peer and brother, while those seduced by the trifles of the sophists <note>In alchemy, "sophists" were fraudulent practitioners or those who misunderstood the natural principles of the craft.</note> may see the light and embrace it, so they might safely return to the right path. Furthermore, I predict that not a few will be enlightened by these labors of mine. These are not fables, but real experiments which I have seen, performed, and known, as the Adept will easily gather from these lines. Therefore, as I write these things for the good of my neighbor, let it suffice that I have professed that no one writing in this art has ever...

*...to have written so clearly, and that while writing I often laid down my pen, because I would have preferred to hide the truth under the mask of envy; but GOD compelled me, whom I could not resist, who alone knows hearts, to whom alone be glory forever. From this I conclude without doubt that many in this final age of the world will be blessed by this secret <note>original: "arcano"</note>. Because I have written faithfully, I have left no doubt for the searching novice <note>original: "Studioso tyroni"; literally a 'studious recruit' or beginner in the art</note> that has not been perfectly satisfied. I also know many who possess this secret along with me, and I am persuaded there are many more, whose friendship I have recently attained anew, so to speak. May the holy will of GOD do what pleases Him; I confess myself unworthy that such things should be accomplished through me: yet in these matters I adore the holy will of GOD, to which all created things are bound to submit, for which alone He created them, and protects them once created.*

# CHAPTER I.
## On the Necessity of Sages' Mercury for the Work of the Elixir.

Whoever desires to possess this <term>Golden Fleece</term> <note>A mythological reference to the prize sought by Jason and the Argonauts, used here to represent the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, let him know that our Gold-making Powder, which we call our <term>Stone</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold.</note>, is Gold only, digested to the highest degree of purity and subtle fixity to which it can be brought by nature and skillful art; which Gold so essentialized is called **Our Gold** (no longer that of the common people <note>original: "vulgi"</note>), and is the final point of the perfection of nature and art. I could cite all the Philosophers on this matter; but I need no witnesses, because I am myself an <term>Adept</term> <note>A practitioner who has successfully completed the "Great Work" and created the Stone.</note>, and I write more clearly than anyone before this. Let him believe who will, let him disprove who can, let him criticize who pleases. He will certainly carry away this reward: deep ignorance. I admit that subtle minds dream of chimeras <note>Illusionary or impossible fantasies.</note>; but the diligent seeker will find the truth in the simple path of nature. Therefore, let Gold be the true, one, and only principle for making gold. Moreover, our Gold is twofold, which we seek for our work, namely mature, fixed, yellow <term>Laton</term> <note>A term for a specific state of the metal, often associated with gold that has been purified but not yet fully "exalted."</note>, whose heart or center is pure fire. Wherefore its body in the fire...

<summary>The author concludes his preface by claiming divine inspiration for revealing alchemical secrets and begins Chapter I by asserting that the Philosopher's Stone is essentially "perfected gold" derived from nature.</summary>
<keywords>Philalethes, Adept, Golden Fleece, Philosopher's Stone, Sages' Mercury, Laton, divine will, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues Chapter I of the treatise "An Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King." The author, Philalethes, is explaining the fundamental nature of the substances required for the "Great Work," specifically focusing on the relationship between the masculine (Gold/Sulfur) and feminine (Mercury) principles.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>An Open Entrance</header>
<meta>catchword: woman</meta>

[the substance] protects it, in which it receives purification, so that nothing yields to its [the fire's] tyranny or suffers at its hands. In our work, this plays the part of the Sea; for this reason, it is joined to our cruder white gold, the female sperm, into which it emits its own sperm, and finally the two unite in an unbreakable bond. Thus is our **Hermaphrodite** <note>A term for the 'Rebis' or the union of opposites—male and female, fixed and volatile—into a single substance.</note> created, possessing the power of both sexes. Therefore, physical gold is "dead" until it is joined with its bride; with her, the coagulating **Sulfur** <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, masculine, or combustible principle that gives form and color.</note>, which is turned outward in Gold, is inverted. In this way, the height is hidden and the depth is revealed. Thus, that which is fixed becomes volatile for a time, so that it may later possess a more noble state by inheritance, in which it attains an overriding stability.

It is clear, therefore, that the whole secret consists in **Mercury** <note>The liquid, passive, or feminine principle. Philalethes is distinguishing between common quicksilver and "Philosophical Mercury."</note>. Regarding this, the Philosopher <note>Likely referring to Aristotle or a high authority like Hermes Trismegistus.</note> says: "In Mercury is everything the Sages seek." Concerning this, Geber <note>A legendary 8th-century Arab alchemist known as the 'Father of Chemistry.'</note> says: "Praise be to the Most High, who created our Mercury and gave it a nature that overcomes all things." Certainly, if this were not present, Alchemists might boast as they wish, but the alchemical work would be in vain. It is evident then that this is no common mercury, but the **Philosophical Mercury** <note>original: "Sophicus"</note>, because all common mercury is Male—that is, physical, specific, and dead—whereas ours is spiritual and female,

<column-break/>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>To the Closed Palace of the King.</header>
<sig>A 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: with</meta>

living and life-giving. Pay attention, therefore, to what I am about to say regarding Mercury, because, as the Philosopher says, our Mercury is the **Salt of the Sages** <note>A metaphorical term for the essential stabilizing principle or the bridge between the volatile and fixed parts of the work.</note>. Anyone who works without it is like an archer who shoots without a bowstring; and yet, it is found nowhere on earth [in its natural state]. The Son, however, is formed by us—not by our act of creation, but by extracting him from those things in which he resides, with nature cooperating in a marvelous way through skillful art.

### ->Regarding Chapter I.<-

This chapter provides us with the definition of the **Gold-making Stone** <note>original: "Lapis Aurificus"</note>, or the exalted essence of Gold itself. This consists in the proper **Solution** <note>The process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state, often called "turning the body into spirit."</note> of the body of Gold by Mercury, and in the **Coagulation** <note>The thickening or solidifying of a liquid into a new solid form.</note> of Mercury by the Sun—that is, the fermenting form of Gold. This operation, however, requires a double Gold: the fixed (that which remains in the fire), which plays the part of the Male. A single ounce of Gold contains a 280th part of seed or **Ferment** <note>The 'yeast' or active agent that triggers the transformation and multiplication of the whole mass.</note> in its heart; the rest does not serve the work. This separation occurs only through crude Gold—that is, our Mercury—which, being alive, restores life to the dead. Thus the depth is revealed—that is, the essence of Gold which lay hidden in its concealed center—is extracted by the Mercury; and the height is hidden—which is the property of Mercury (which, by its volatile nature, rises on high). This volatile nature hides itself; that is, it is fixed through coagulation by the active form of the Gold. Therefore, the entire intention in this single

<summary>This page defines "Philosophical Mercury" as a life-giving female principle essential for dissolving "dead" physical gold. It explains that the Philosopher's Stone is created by extracting a tiny "ferment" from the heart of gold and fixing it into a new, stable form using this specialized Mercury.</summary>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the first chapter of Philalethes’ treatise, transitioning from a discussion on the union of the "Hermaphrodite" (the combined alchemical substance) to the specific preparation of the "Philosophical Mercury" required for the Great Work.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>An Open Entrance.</header>

...since it is necessary to explain how this **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurius"; refers here to the specialized "Philosophical Mercury," not the common quicksilver found in thermometers.</note> must be prepared: For Gold is extremely hot, while Mercury is exceedingly cold. These two cannot be joined to one another fundamentally except through a medium that shares the nature of both. For all common Mercury is physical—that is, it is "dead"—and possesses no power to bring life back. Therefore, a "hot form" must necessarily be introduced into it, in which Gold may melt. This Essence, which is to be united with Mercury, is called the **Salt of the Philosophers** <note>original: "Sal Philosophorum"; a metaphor for the catalytic agent that "sharpens" the mercury to make it reactive with gold.</note>, because Mercury is sharpened by it just as common water is by salt; therefore, it rightly claims the name of Salt. This Essence, created by God, is hidden within the nature of metals and is extracted by the Seekers of the Art with great labor and ingenuity, as will become clearer from what is said below.

## CHAPTER II.
### Concerning the Component Principles of the Sages' Mercury.

The intention of some who work in this art is to purify Mercury in various ways: some, by adding salts, **sublime** <note>To heat a substance so it turns to vapor and then becomes solid again, a common purification method.</note> it to remove various dregs; others attempt to "quicken" it by itself. Thus, through repeated operations, they claim to have created the **Mercury of the Philosophers**—and they err, because they do not work within nature, which alone can be improved within its own nature. Let them know, therefore,

<meta>catchword: that,</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>To the Closed Palace of the King.</header>
<sig>A 4</sig>

that our **Water** <note>Alchemical "Water" is a code for the universal solvent or the prepared Mercury.</note> is composed of many things, yet it is a single thing made from different substances grown together into one Essence. In our Water, there is required: first, **Fire**; second, the liquid of **vegetable Saturnia** <note>original: "Saturnia vegetabilis"; likely a reference to a specific state of antimony or a "living" lead-derived substance used as a solvent.</note>; third, the **Bond of Mercury**. The Fire is a mineral **Sulfur**, and yet it is not strictly mineral, much less metallic; rather, it is a medium between ore and metal, a neutral thing participating in both—a **Chaos** or Spirit. For our **Fiery Dragon** <note>original: "Draco Noster igneus"; a symbol for the volatile, acidic, or heat-producing principle.</note>, which overcomes all things, is nonetheless pierced by the scent of the vegetable Saturnia; its blood grows together with the juice of Saturnia into one marvelous Body. And yet it is not a body, because it is entirely volatile; nor is it a spirit, because in the fire it resembles a melted metal. It is, therefore, truly a **Chaos**, which stands as a Mother to all metals. For from it, I have learned to extract all things—even the **Sun** (Gold) and **Moon** (Silver) without a transmuting Elixir—as whoever has seen it can testify. This Chaos is called our **Arsenic**, our **Air**, our **Moon**, our **Magnet**, and our **Steel** <note>Alchemists often used the names of common minerals (Arsenic, Steel) as metaphors for the properties of their secret substance (e.g., "Steel" for the thing that attracts the "Magnet").</note>—though in different respects, because our matter undergoes various states before the royal crown is separated from the **menstrual blood of our harlot** <note>original: "meretricis nostræ Menstruo"; a provocative metaphor for the raw, unrefined starting material or the solvent in its active state.</note>. Learn, therefore, who are the companions of **Cadmus** <note>In Greek myth, Cadmus sowed dragon's teeth; here it refers to the chemical reactions involved in breaking down the "Dragon."</note>, and what is the **Serpent** that devoured them, and what is the **Hollow Oak**, to...

<meta>catchword: which</meta>

<summary>Philalethes explains that common mercury is "dead" and must be activated by a "Salt of the Sages" to bond with gold. He describes the "Philosophical Water" as a composite "Chaos" that acts as the mother of all metals, using dense mythological allegories like the Fiery Dragon and Cadmus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Chapter II, transitioning from the theoretical requirements of the Sages' Mercury to the specific mythological allegories used to describe its preparation and the chemical substances involved.</meta>
